ParlaMint-DK_20150430100756	The debate is open. It's the Minister of Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430100815	This time, the Liberal Alliance seems to want to phase out the holiday money system altogether. In fact, at the request of the Liberal Alliance back in spring 2010, Parliament had a debate on the future of the holiday law. At that time a broad majority in the Folketing agreed that the purpose of the holiday law is to protect the safety and health of employees and that it is therefore important that employees take holidays for their holiday money. At that time it was agreed that the holiday law provides satisfactory protection for workers. Denmark's international obligations, economic considerations and the balance of the collective agreement system make it difficult to change the fundamentals of the principles of the holiday law. Denmark is actually obliged to comply with the international rules, which are also about holidays. Those rules set a limit on how much we can change the holiday law at national level. There are, for example: the following restrictions: Agreement on the waiver of leave is invalid; that is to say, you have to take your leave; the employee is entitled to four weeks' paid leave per year; and the holiday allowance may not be paid directly to the employee unless the employment relationship ceases. For this reason alone, it is not possible to pay the holiday money together with the salary, so the Liberal Alliance's proposal is in fact contrary to the international obligations of Denmark. In addition, I would like to make it clear that it is crucial for the government that we have a holiday money system which is guarding the opportunities for Danish employees and also their right to take holidays. It is a basic condition for paying your earned holiday money that you actually take your holiday. The main rule is that holiday money is lost to employees if they do not take a holiday during the holiday year. Instead, the holiday money is mainly allocated to the Labour Market Holiday Fund and perhaps also to some private funds. It then ensures, on the other hand, that employees are encouraged to take holidays during the holiday year. It also ensures that the employer cannot make any money out of the fact that the employee is not taking his or her vacation. In some cases, however, there is the possibility of receiving a holiday allowance without taking a holiday, for example, if the employee has been prevented from taking a holiday because of illness or maternity up to the end of the holiday period or up to the end of the year. Unclaimed holiday allowances may also be paid at the end of the holiday year if the holiday allowance relates either to the fifth week of leave or if it has ceased employment. In addition, the Danish holiday law is to a large extent an integral part of collective agreements and thus also part of the balance between the social partners. The holiday area is characterised by a long tradition that the social partners jointly find solutions for the benefit of both employees and employers. I believe that the result of the Liberal Alliance's proposal could be that hard-working people do not have a holiday at all and thus suffer even more quickly. This is not consistent with the fact that we are at the same time demanding that employees stay longer and longer in the labour market. Society therefore also has an important interest in actually having a holiday. On the other hand, the government would like to see ongoing opportunities to make the holiday law more flexible. I would like to take this opportunity to mention the new system called Holiday Money Information, which will be operational here from 1 May 2015. Holiday money info is a portal which now allows employees to have a comprehensive overview of their savings in holiday allowances. Here, too, you can ask for your holiday money. So many of the points raised by the Liberal Alliance along the way have actually been accepted, and I think that is sensible, so I hardly have to say in conclusion that the government cannot support the Liberal Alliance's motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430101431	Thank you to the Minister for Employment. Sir. Hans Andersen as rapporteur for the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430101445	Thank you. The motion for a resolution raises a recurring problem, namely that not all employees are paid their holiday money and that all employees are thereby also deprived of interest. In the motion for a resolution, Liberal Alliance notes that holiday money is part of the wage and proposes that it be paid on a regular basis by the employer together with the salary, so that the interest is paid to the employee himself. The current holiday money system has worked for many years, and it is an integral part of the Danish holiday law. Nevertheless, not all employees are paid holiday allowances. This is certainly not satisfactory and it would suggest that we could look at the possibilities of improving this holiday money system so that all employees would receive their own earned money. However, a proposal for a permanent payment of the holiday allowance to employees is one that I consider to be contrary to the EU Working Time Directive and to the practice of the courts, because according to the case law of the European Court of Justice, the holiday allowance cannot be paid together with the salary and the employee must receive his or her normal salary when the employee is taking his or her holiday. So if we are to modernise the ability of employees to have their own paid holiday money paid in an easy way, we need to clarify how this can be done while complying with the EU's working time directive, and this is not the case with this resolution. However, we would very much like to see this clarified in order to ensure that all employees, i.e. the individual employees, receive their own paid holiday money while complying with the EU Working Time Directive. Then, of course, it is also a challenge for companies today, with the current holiday money system, to have a not insignificant contribution to their liquidity, and we must also take that into account when and if we change the holiday money system. So we think that we should think about a solution and work on a solution that ensures that the individual employee gets his own holiday money paid out, and maybe that could happen and I say perhaps because I think it requires a good job through an automatic payout. In other words, if you have not raised your holiday money after a certain period, this could perhaps be done by means of an automatic payment, so that we ensured that all employees were paid their own holiday money. But that is not what this proposal is all about, and on that basis the Left cannot support the specific motion for a resolution. However, we would like to look at how to ensure that the individual employee receives his or her own holiday money. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430102037	As the labour market rapporteur, you are clearly faced with having an opinion on many things, and it is really only the imagination that limits the ingenuity of issues and themes. For the Social Democrats, it is important for an employee in Denmark to take a holiday, and it is not only a matter of dancing on the beach, but also of central health concerns. This is ensured by the Act on Holidays and by a very long tradition that the social partners jointly find solutions that benefit both employees and employers. Today we have a holiday money system which, on the one hand, encourages Danish employees to take their holidays during holiday periods and, on the other hand, ensures that it is not possible for employers to make money from the fact that their employees are not on holiday. These are two pillars of the rules that will be lost if this resolution is adopted. In addition, the proposal is directly contrary to Denmark's international obligations, which clearly state that holiday allowances may not be paid directly to the employee unless the working conditions stop. But the most important thing is perhaps the consideration of the Danish labour market model. We have a long tradition in Denmark that the social partners jointly agree a number of conditions on the Danish labour market. This is particularly true in the field of holidays. This ensures that the rules are beneficial to both employers and workers, without interfering with Cristiansborg policy. It is a good labour market model in general and it is a good labour market model in the field of holidays. It is the position of the Social Democrats to stand firm on that model. Having said all that, it is of course important that the rules on the payment of holiday money also comply with the political intentions of the scheme, which is why it is also the view of the Socialists that the rules should be constantly monitored and that they should be considered whether they can be better done in favour of both employees and employers in a way that does not compromise with the Danish model. This motion for a resolution does not meet those requirements in any way. Here we are only at risk of the employees not having a well-deserved holiday, which is neither for the benefit of employees, employers or Danish society, and therefore the Social Democratic Party cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430103518	Thank you to the radical rapporteur. There are no brief comments. Then it's mr. Eigil Andersen as SF rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430103535	Many people benefit from the fact that the current system of holiday money ensures that the money is present when the holiday is to be held. With the proposal of the Liberal Alliance, there are quite a few people who will be left without a penny to take a holiday for when they get a holiday, and that is simply because it will be too tempting for a number of people to spend the money continuously, not least if you have a poor economy. It is a temptation that one can easily understand. But more broadly speaking, the truth is that people are very different, and therefore the ability of individual people to save is very different. I think it would be very bad if a large group of citizens were to arrive at their holidays and then not have a penny to hold it for. In particular, I would like to point out that in Danish society a great many employees are still absent who are not white-collar workers, and if they are going to spend their holiday money during the year, they will simply have no money to pay rent while they are on holiday, because non-members do not receive a penny in wages during the holiday, not a penny, but they will then receive a higher amount of holiday money. So there may simply be some who are put out of their apartment because they have not kept the holiday money until the holiday is to be held and therefore it is a bad system. Approx. 75% of all holiday allowances in Denmark are paid through the so-called holiday card schemes. Vacation card schemes have been agreed in collective bargaining with trade unions and have been approved by the State. The holiday card scheme is a kind of guarantee scheme administered by employers' associations, which means that the individual employer has the interest rate advantage by keeping the money in the company until the holiday is taken. On the other hand, the employers' association guarantees that the holiday money is always available when the holiday is to be taken. This increases the liquidity of Danish private companies by about 12 billion. kr. a year, the Danish Employers' Association, which I have spoken to. If employers were to borrow this money from the bank, according to the employers' association, it would cost private companies between 500 million. kr. and 1 billion. kr. a year. I am very surprised that the Liberal Alliance is attacking business in this way by removing an advantage of between 0.5 billion. kr. and 1 billion. kr. per year for enterprises. I thought that the Liberal Alliance as a Saxo Bank spree was a business-friendly party, but one can then make it clear in this Chamber that the Liberal Alliance is not. As far as the SF is concerned, it is an even more central aspect that, if the current system, agreed in the collective bargaining process, is to be removed, it is to be expected that employers will make demands in the next collective bargaining negotiations, which will bring them back the billion kroner demanded by the Liberal Alliance from employers. And that can be done, for example. In contrast, at the next collective bargaining procedure, employers will require employees to reduce wages, or in the industrial sector, where the agreement states that they are entitled to two weeks of paid training per year, it can be imagined that employers will demand that this right be abolished in return for the disappearance of the holiday money system. With the kind of expectable demands from employers which lead to deterioration in other areas, employees are therefore equal and, therefore, we cannot see from the SF that the Liberal Alliance's proposal is a step forward. The proposal is further proof that the liberalist theories do not stand up in practice and therefore the SF will vote against the proposal. And I would like to conclude by saying that we would like to take part in efforts to ensure that more employees raise their holiday money. It is possible to find out with the help of various tools, but it must be an absolute condition that the holiday is also taken.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430104437	When we have to make sure we have the opportunity to take a holiday, there are two things that need to be right. The first is the right to vacation, which we have in part in the holiday law and in part in a number of agreements which have extended the law on holidays. And the second is to have some money to take the holidays too. We have three kinds of holiday money, as I see it. We have the white-collar workers who get paid on vacation. Then we have ordinary workers who get holiday money from saved holidays. And then we have the third option, that we have something called holiday allowance. This means that if you have spent the previous year on unemployment benefit, you can also receive holiday allowance the following year or if you are on unemployment benefit, you can receive holiday allowance and take your holiday there. It's a good combination, but not a combination without holes. But there are some advantages of the current system that the Liberal Alliance has to answer questions about before they skip them off by making this model. That is why it is important that, if we are to discuss this in a nuanced way, we also have to answer those questions. The Vacation Act is a legal guarantee for the Danish employees, namely their right to take a holiday and receive a salary during the holiday. If you remove the holiday money as legal claims, the only ones who will benefit from it are the employers, in particular the employers who cheat, each wage earner must make claims to his employer through his trade union or, if you are not in a trade union, through civil law. In my opinion, the law is one of the world's best in the sense that it prevents employers from cheating on holiday money because they have to be paid out on a quarterly basis. There is a public system which ensures that they are paid and if they are not paid, a case will be brought against the employer. The holiday money ensures that all wage-earners can afford to take a holiday, not because it is paternal, but because the money is found in a savings that is legally guaranteed. And as I said before, we're sure to get the money in. I have also met families who have been wearing their hair in the mailbox because they had no more money left because they had traded with the employer. It has been a great pleasure to drive out to the same employer and say. You cheated on the holiday money, and I'd like it back, you might say. I'd like them officially. There are a number of employers who are then caught up and have had to pay holiday money twice because they have not followed the legislation. That is their problem, and we have put it in place that way. We do not share the Liberal Alliance's view that the state is paternalistic in securing holiday money, on the contrary. If the holiday money system is abolished and the money instead becomes part of the salary, this alone will result in a reduction in wages and the weakest workers being cheated. However, we believe that unspent holiday money should be possible after the end of the holiday year, and that holiday money should not be offset against other social benefits. This does not mean the abolition of holiday money, but the strengthening of workers' rights in the law, and we would like to take part in that. If you could get into that situation, we think it would be really, really good to get in place.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430105118	In Denmark we have a system in which we seize a very large proportion of wages in income tax. You can have different views about this, but you choose to seize 12.5% on top of it. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the European Parliament's role in this debate is to make sure that people's salaries are paid in order to ensure that they are now also on holiday is completely insane. People are worried about people who can't figure out how to save up for vacation, but how many are they really about? When the few have problems in managing their finances, one should not demand forced savings for everyone. Then, on the basis of the same logic, one could demand government compulsory savings for rent, for Christmas gifts or perhaps for all kinds of other necessities. Today we have an entirely bizarre system in which employees have to stand with their hats in their hands to gain access to their own money. Either you have to ask the employer to confirm that you are going to take a holiday and ask for your money, or after the holiday year you can apply for access to your own salary if you have either been on holiday or have been prevented from keeping it. That system is a crazy guardianship. The aim of the legislation is to ensure that people take 5 weeks' holiday. Is there anyone who can rationally justify that all people in Denmark need 5 weeks' holiday? If you are in doubt that the law is indeed an expression of paternalism, you can consult the legislative text. The Vacation Act says: "Of the holiday, at least 15 days must be given in context (main holiday). The main holiday is to be held between 1 May and 30 September (the holiday period). If the employee has earned less than 15 days' holiday, the entire earned holiday is the main holiday. It gets comical when you read on: • An employee who is engaged in outdoor plant breeding must have at least 10 days in conjunction with the holiday period. In addition, at least 5 days must be given between 1 May and 31 October. ▪ What, frankly, is the good argument for Christiansborg to regulate the holiday period for people working with outdoor plant breeding? There is no good argument for this in the same way as there are no good arguments for people to spend 15 days of continuous holiday in the summer period. If someone prefers to take vacation at other times, they must of course find out with their employer, not with the politicians in here. The success of the present holiday system is due to an unholy alliance between the employers' associations, the state and the trade unions. In the case of ordinary employees, on average they are cheated on the interest rates which ought to be due to them. After all, the holiday money is part of the salary. And especially are those who do not get the right paperwork so that they can get paid the missing part of their salary. If it is forgotten, or if you do not have five weeks' holiday, then suddenly you are not entitled to the money you have saved and earned yourself. It is simply theft from ordinary employees. The employers' associations, the state and the trade unions earn a lot of money on this. If a collective agreement is drawn up, companies can avoid the rules that apply to the holiday account. For companies without collective agreement, employees' savings pay must be paid on a regular basis to the Holiday Account. As a member of e.g. A collective agreement avoids the need to pay holiday allowances on an ongoing basis. It provides a liquidity advantage. That is a nice way of saying that employers are snatching interest income. DI itself states that a conservative estimate of the savings for the companies is that they are approx. 0.26% of the wages and salaries. The price of being a member of DI is, by chance, 0.26%. of the wages and salaries. It is, of course, a fun coincidence that many employees pay exactly for companies being able to join the employers' organisations, for their holiday money, and it is a fun coincidence that the same organisations are in favour of the current holiday money system. In relation to the state, things are even clearer. The State has granted itself a large part of the interest on holiday money, which previously belonged to the Labour Market Holiday Fund, and this is not insignificant in structural terms when the total amount of holiday money is as large as it is. At the same time, in the last financial year 2013, more than 50 million were taken. kr. directly in the profit and loss account. This was while the 2014 Finance Act decided to include 280 million. kr. of the own funds of the Labour Market Holiday Fund. It's shameful. On the sidelines, the trade unions are passively watching while their members' salaries are being eroded. The trade unions are more busy ensuring that all employees receive exactly five weeks' leave than ensuring that they receive their entire salary together with the interest rate of that salary. It's a clear failure. An increasingly smaller proportion of the profits from this whole circus goes to the Labour Market Holiday Fund. This is intended to support holiday stays for children and families in need. It seems to most people as a sympathetic purpose, but whether it is still worth supporting must always be based on a political priority that must compete with all the other good purposes for which money is intended. But it is unsympathetic to allow it to finance in the holiday money system as an unfair special tax on the diligent and on those who have forgotten to ask for their own money to be paid out. If we want to continue to ensure that people take holidays at the times and in the amounts prescribed by Christiansborg, we should at least make sure that there is a dispute with the present system, which ensures that neither the State nor the employers give themselves a chunk of the money which must in every way belong to the employees. Other things are to care for special interests without thinking of ordinary justice. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430111614	Thank you, Minister. Then it's mr. Preben Bang Henriksen as rapporteur for the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430111628	Thank you. The motion for a resolution we are debating today, tabled by the Enhedslisten, concerns the creation of an ethics regime to tell the consumer whether the product has been produced in accordance with fundamental workers' rights and human rights, etc. More specifically, it is a question of the secretariat and the board that will operate under it here, among other things. to consider compliance with the Geneva Convention, the Declaration of Human Rights and the Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, economic, social and cultural rights, the guidelines on human rights and professions; that recruitment has been voluntary; that there is a safe and healthy working environment; that there is no child labour; that there is freedom of association; that there is no excessive working hours; that there is no discrimination in employment; and that there is a wage guaranteeing an adequate standard of living. All of these requirements must be taken into account in all the managers of the production process, that is, from the raw material's supply and processing and later transport. In addition, it is stressed that it is both the producer and the subcontractors who have to meet these requirements. I have to say at the outset that we are dealing with a proper mouthful, and this is obviously a matter of all goods. At the same time, I have also noted that it must be financed by the Treasury, without any amount of money being set aside for it. We in the Left believe that it is important for consumers to have relevant information so that they can make choices according to their wishes and needs. Through their choices, consumers are actively influencing goods and services on the market, and we are constantly watching that. Already today there is a market for products that meet different ethical requirements, so consumers actually have an option today. There are examples of private brand schemes, for example what is known as the Max Havelaar scheme. We in the Left believe that an ethical labelling system should be driven by companies' involvement in cooperation with consumers. It is not for the State to be a taster of the ethical criteria to be emphasised. We should not create an apparatus of so-called taste judges to assess what good ethics are. Ethics, as you know, can be many things, and any real formula of what good ethics is, is not known to me. I think it should be left to the discretion of the individual consumer to decide what should be the most important emphasis on whether it is environmentally friendly, human rights, animal welfare or whatever we might talk about. So the Left does not want to contribute to the establishment of this system and, overall, the Left cannot support the scheme proposed.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430112145	Thank you to the rapporteur of the Left. Then it is Mrs Mette Reissmann as Social Democrat rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430112155	I would like to start by expressing my appreciation of the fact that the Enhedslisten raises this issue here in the Assembly. To fight for workers' rights both in Denmark and in the rest of the world is social democratic heartblood; it is a battle that we must always fight. In the past, major tragedies, particularly in the textile industry, have highlighted how these products we consume in the West are produced and what conditions the people who produce them are working under. It is not funny reading and it has unfortunately been shown that working conditions, especially in developing countries, are highly critical. Therefore, it is also relevant to discuss how we can get companies and producers to give their employees proper working conditions. And that is why, as early as 2013, we presented a CSR action plan focusing on social responsibility in both the private sector and consumers. The action plan contains some tools and principles, especially for Danish companies operating in new growth markets where human rights are often under pressure. In addition, in the wake of the tragedy in Bangladesh, where a textile factory collapsed, the government launched a series of additional measures to ensure more responsible production and working conditions during the production of these products. And that was among other things. initiatives such as CSR Abroad, increased focus on CSR under EU-trade agreements and a targeted collaboration with Dansk Erhverv. It is all initiatives that will make Danish as well as foreign companies take responsibility for working conditions and workers' rights, both in their own factories, with their subcontractors and in society where they place their production. I should also like to mention that the Socialist Group in the European Parliament, among other things, is in fact one of them. As far back as 1999, when we were last in government, we had a labelling report that has highlighted this problem. We had the then business strategy, dk21, which resulted in the ethics database, which I had the great pleasure of being the leader of in the years of the existing one. So this is not the first time that the Socialists have, as it were, been involved in this issue. So the Socialists are very much in agreement with the Enhedslisten that we have a share of responsibility for ensuring proper conditions throughout the production chain. We just do not believe that the Enhedslisten's proposed ethics mark is the right solution to the problem, because in a modern consumer jungle, where there is already a host of different brands, there is only one thing that can confuse consumers further, and that is to get another mark. That is why, although consumers are generally happy with brands, we believe that this is not the right way to do it. We will always work for transparency and consumer information. These are necessary tools if consumers are to take co-responsibility precisely in the fight against poor working conditions. This, of course, requires you to know what you are buying if you, as a consumer, are to make a difference with your choice of consumption. Having said that, however, the criterion of social responsibility is already included in several of our eco-labellings. There are a number of tools that the Minister has mentioned some of those that allow companies to advertise with it when they take social responsibility. That is why we do not believe that the economic consequences and the extensive work involved in setting up a new labelling system, and indeed the administration of it, are in line with the benefits. Labelling is one way of getting consumers to make an active choice, but it requires clarity and credibility about the different brands, and for many different brands it may risk losing them. So we simply do not believe that this proposal is the best way to ensure proper working conditions and therefore the Socialists cannot support the proposal.

That is to say, as a consumer, you are left on the land of the gentleman. That's not good. The political consumer really has a great influence, fortunately I think. We have seen that when, for example, we see it. I've been talking about organic products. Lots of people want to buy organic products because they want to avoid spray poison, as we all do by polluting our drinking water, and it is probably not very healthy to eat products that have been sprayed. We have also seen what has also been said from the platform, with brands such as Max Havelaar Fairtrade, where it is ensured that plantation workers, whether they are now concerned with getting us coffee or bananas, have had proper working conditions and that they are in any case sustainable production. This is something that many consumers are looking for, so it means that there is a great need for information. I believe that it should ideally be the case if one could arrive at that, that when I stand in a supermarket and have to choose from some goods, I should be able to see whether it has been produced under some conditions that give fair rights to those workers who have worked to produce them and who also ensure that there is sustainability in relation to all the environmental issues there are. So in this way we believe in SF that Enhedslisten's proposal contains a basically very good idea. Then various points have been made in the debate. That is, for example, the case. has been said that we have the United Nations system of Global Compact. That is also true, and it is also an excellent system. Here firms can report that they comply with ten principles, which are absolutely excellent principles, and then they can live up to their social responsibilities in a very good way. It is then a voluntary scheme that companies can join, but it is rather different from making a mark on products manufactured under these conditions. So I do not think that the Global Compact scheme in itself is an answer to the proposal put forward by Enhedslisten here. It has also been mentioned that the proposal provides for the labelling system to be financed by the State. That's not exactly what it says. It states that the labelling scheme must be state-funded or state-supported. It's an opening. It may be that it must be State-supported without specifying the amount of aid to be granted by the State. After all, the proposal envisages that there may be a discussion about how large or how small this state aid should be. It is clear that there must be some balance in it, so that the cost involved does not go up in the sky. But I do believe that many things can be done, also on the basis of the idea that Enhedslisten has put forward here. It is quite right, as has been said here in the debate, that it should be the case that it is also something that we are working on internationally. So true, so true! But the point is that there are a number of situations where it is important that some countries take the lead. It has also been Denmark on a number of occasions that has set the good example that can inspire other countries and that can perhaps inspire the European Union and in fact also inspire the United Nations. So that is the way to see political developments as a process. You cannot always get the big chromed solution from the start, and then you have to say, like the pragmatist I think most of us are, that we must start with what can be done and then try to work on it. So in this way the SF is positive about Enhedslisten's proposal and can vote for it.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430130647	Thank you. When we enter a supermarket or shop in Denmark, the goods we find on the shelves are often produced under intolerable and directly dangerous conditions for the worker. This is the case in the textile industry, where over 1,100 workers died a few years ago during the collapse of Rana Plaza, a textile factory in Bangladesh, while they were sewing clothes for consumers in Europe and the United States. This also applies to Indian cotton farmers who commit suicide because they are so poor that they cannot make everyday life hang together. This applies to Colombian coffee farmers and miners who risk being killed if they organize themselves in a union. And this is very much the case with the workers who produce computers, frying pans, cars and all the other things we use in everyday life. Today, the consumer often has no way of knowing how the product is produced. Businesses can keep consumers in ignorance of or directly hide that they do not respect basic labour rights, use child labour and fire professionally active. The growing relocation of production has in many cases led to a race to the bottom. Companies place their production where it is cheapest to produce. All too often consideration of basic labour rights comes to give way to the desire for low production costs. I do not think we should just close our eyes to the fact that the clothes we wear, the computer we have here, or the cup of coffee we drink, have been produced under inhuman conditions. We must do something about this in many ways, both by strengthening legislation and by strengthening the international trade union movement and civil society organisations. However, we must also make it easier for consumers who want to make a responsible and solidarity choice when they act. That is why we are proposing the creation of an ethical label to enable consumers to choose products which have been produced in good conditions for the workers and which show that basic labour rights and human rights are respected in the production of the goods. There are already good examples; the fair trade mark is mentioned, and it covers foods such as bananas, coffee and chocolate and provides good guidance to consumers and follows up on production. There will also be more and more labelling in the textile sector. But on a great many products the consumer is currently left on the land of the gentleman. This is everything from frying pans to cars where the consumer really does not have the opportunity to make a responsible choice. An ethical label that can be used by producers selling in Denmark will, in the same way as eco-labels and eco-labels, allow consumers to choose products produced under proper conditions and to opt out of products produced under inhuman conditions. At the same time, this will give companies an increased incentive to respect workers' rights, as opposed to today, when it is all too often a good business to override workers' rights. It is an essential task to ensure proper control and thus the legitimacy of such a label, and it is therefore necessary for the state to be prepared to invest in making an ethics mark work. There are several precedents for labelling schemes which have received public support and which work very well: The eco-labelled Swan and Flower, managed by the Danish Standard and the Ministry of the Environment, have ensured that consumers can choose more environmentally friendly products. With this proposal, we would like to look at the possibility of creating an ethical label, such as: are linked to the Ministry of Trade and Development and where a group or board is set up to administer the label, consisting of representatives of ministries, trade unions, consumer organisations, human rights organisations, retail trade and professions, in short, operators already working in the area. One could, for example: organise the establishment of an ethics secretariat dealing with applications from companies wishing to have the ethics mark on their products and monitoring compliance with the criteria. The criteria for obtaining the label on its goods must include the product being produced in compliance with international law, UN human rights and UN guidelines on human rights and professions and fundamental labour rights based on ILO conventions. The requirements shall apply at all stages of the production process, i.e.: for both the supplier and the chain of subcontractors. Monitoring and assessment of compliance shall be carried out in cooperation with relevant trade unions, civil society organisations and authorities in the country of production. It will also be obvious to involve the Mediation and Complaint Institution for Responsible Corporate Conduct, the OECD Contact Point, which we have in Denmark. I hope that the Folketing will contribute to the establishment of an ethical label for Denmark, because it will help consumers who want to choose goods that are produced properly. This will make it more attractive for companies that want to improve conditions, and it is an absolutely necessary contribution to our joint responsibility to improve the working conditions of the billions of people who produce our clothes, computers and cups of coffee. Unfair conditions have already outperformed thousands of Danish jobs, although I recognise that a number of Danish companies have found their way to profit from the miserable conditions, for example. in the textile industry. So in this way I would like to recommend the proposal and I thank you for the contributions. I see no problem, as the Left points out, namely that it should be a great monstrum of an expensive administrative apparatus. Other labelling schemes have found a natural rental, and in cooperation with the industry, of course, the bill must also be paid by those who have a profit from such a labelling scheme. It is not a purely state expense, and we have not written that in the proposal either. In addition to that, it is surely a minimum, we should be able to do that in Denmark, and we can start a process which can then be brought to the European Union and which can be brought to the United Nations, so that we can make the scheme a good addition to the initiatives already taken by the trade union movement and the UN.

In my view, this minor breakthrough also points to the fact that the right solution is not an extension of the tax exemption for four years. Four years is a long time, and a technological breakthrough, together with a tax exemption, can quickly lead to large State revenues and an unfortunate competitive advantage for electric cars compared with other vehicles. The Enhedslisten proposes to finance an extension of the tax exemption by unspent funds from the current tax exemption. I'm afraid that's not an option. ParlaMint-DK_20150430145336	Thank you. The Liberal Alliance has put forward a proposal to reduce the registration fee by 1%. and finance the reduction of the petrol and diesel tax by 1%. Car taxes are indeed high in this country, but they have now fallen considerably in recent years. The revenue for the registration fee is currently approx. 8 billion. kr. lower than it was in 2007, and that is, although the sale of cars has been record high for the last 4-5 years. I think it was not quite intended when the previous government changed the registration fee that this should happen, but I think it is quite reasonable that Danish drivers, among other things, should do so. contribute to the financing of roads and, for example, to the financing of public transport. I also think it is reasonable that they help to pay for the costs for the environment, for the climate and for the health of drivers. Driving leads to CO2 emissions It is true that the Danish car taxes are overall high when we compare with other countries, but exactly the taxes on petrol and diesel, which are also some of the things mentioned in the proposal, are actually lower in Denmark than they are, for example, in Sweden and Germany. The tax on petrol in Denmark is only slightly above the average rates across the EU, while the diesel tax is largely on the EU average. When the prices of petrol and diesel in Denmark are at or above prices in Sweden and Germany, this is not due to higher Danish taxes, but to higher costs and profit margins in Denmark. It is the assessment that foreigners cross-border trade more fuel in Denmark than we cross-border trade fuel abroad. And foreigners would probably cross-border trade more in Denmark if the margins on fuel were not so high in this country. The high vehicle taxes in Denmark are mainly due to the high registration fee. The high tax contributes to fewer cars and to smaller cars, and that benefits the climate, the environment and health. A general reduction in the registration fee, as well as in petrol and diesel taxes, should therefore also be expected to lead to more cars, to larger cars and to more driving. This will increase our emissions of CO2 It is also to be expected to lead to more accidents and more congestion, and this goes against our general objectives in climate and environmental matters. I would also like to point out the myth that cars in Denmark should be significantly older than cars in the countries with which we usually compare ourselves. In the other Nordic countries, including Sweden, the average age of the car park is e.g. higher than in Denmark. The average age of cars in Denmark has been decreasing in recent years, and at the same time we can see that there is a large increase in the number of newly registered passenger cars, while the car park has not grown to the same extent. It points out that there is a large replacement of the oldest cars with new cars at the moment and that in many cases it is worth replacing an old car with a new one. It hangs, among other things. together with the fact that we have a car tax system that has built in strong economic incentives to choose cars with a low CO2 although the registration fee is high, it has been decreasing in recent years, among other things. in view of the reductions made in the fuel economy, which, together with technological developments, have helped to improve energy efficiency and, overall, contributed to a decrease. New and energy-efficient cars therefore have a major competitive advantage. The Environmental Economic Council, to which the proposers refer, also concluded in their 2013 report that for the smallest cars fuel taxes correspond roughly to the cost of driving. I do not think there is a need for further reductions in car charges than those which are automatically built in the form of an incentive to choose the most fuel efficient cars, and certainly not a general reduction of 1%. As regards the financing of the proposal, the proposers state that a 1% reduction in the petrol and diesel tax. According to the Department of Taxation's own calculations, the reduction in registration fees can be financed by 1%. In this context, the proposers refer to a reply to the Danish Parliament's Tax Committee delivered in June 2013. However, the self-financing rate for both the registration fee and the fuel charges has been updated since then on the basis of more recent data bases. The Danish Parliament's Tax Committee is also informed about this. And with the current estimate of the level of self-financing, the proposal would not be self-financing. A 1% reduction in diesel and petrol taxes. It also means that the so-called countervailing charge on diesel cars must be reduced by 1% if the balance between taxes on diesel and petrol vehicles is to be maintained, and this results in a further reduction in revenue. However, there is uncertainty about the calculations and self-financing rates, and this also illustrates the updates. But no matter what, lower taxes are expected to lead to more cars and more driving. This is against the government's objectives in the climate and environment sectors, and the government cannot therefore support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430150324	After all, it is no secret that Danish drivers pay very high taxes. It is more than 40 billion a year. The Danish drivers spit in the box. If you are in the situation of taxation that taxes are very, very high, then there is indeed a risk or possibility that a tax has become so high that if you lower the tax rate, revenue can actually increase. That is popularly said what is called the luffer basket, which shows that if you have a zero tax rate, you get no revenue, and if you have a tax rate of 100, the activity stops, and then nothing gets into the tax either. And somewhere in between there is then a peak where you get the maximum revenue from a given tax rate. What is the problem is, of course, if the tax rates are so high that revenue is lower than if the tax rate is reduced. It is not unknown that we have established it. That has been a few years ago, but I have been involved in dealing with a legislative proposal in the Parliamentary Assembly, where we reduced a rate of packaging tax and budgeted to get more money simply because the tax rate was too high and by lowering the tax rate we actually received a higher revenue. This is the same problem that the motion for a resolution by the Liberal Alliance is attacking, and with the references in the proposal, the self-financing rate of the registration fee is 95 per cent. This means that if the figures are up to the believers now the tax minister says that there are more recent figures, and we must get that fully lit up then it is a very, very small loss of revenue that will be reducing the registration fee by a single percentage, namely 5 million. kr. ParlaMint-DK_20150430154746	Thank you, President. The Danish People's Party will by B 128 require the government to make it an obligation for the municipalities to provide information about their processing times on the municipal websites. In fact, I agree that citizens must have good access to knowledge and, in other words, to know what level of service they can expect in terms of processing time in their municipality. It is precisely for this reason that the law on legal certainty already provides that each municipality must set general time limits for the municipality's proceedings in the individual cases and that these time limits must be published. It is, as the proposers of course know, left to the individual municipal council to decide how to publish it. In my optics it will be very natural to publish the municipal deadlines on the municipality's website. Fortunately, many municipalities do. But one thing is to think that the website is an appropriate medium, another is to ask the Danish Parliament to lay down legal rules for all municipalities to publish their local deadlines in precisely that way. Citizens can exercise their local democratic influence if they want a particular or a changed form of publication in their municipality. They can, for example. do in the local public debate or through disability council or senior council in the municipality. This is a local subject where there is no reason for the Folketing to impose a particular method or form on the municipalities. The rules we have today already provide for the consideration of citizens and the citizens' quite reasonable and natural need to know what processing time they can expect from their local authority in each area. The government cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430160701	Thank you for that, President. It is the view of the Danish People's Party that it is important for citizens to be able to keep up with the processing times in the municipality in which they live. It must be easy and simple to keep track of the times in the social field, and citizens must be able to see how much time it takes to deal with a matter. Article 3 (3) The law on legal certainty and administration in the social field states that the municipal council must set time limits for the period between the receipt of an application and the decision. The time limits shall be published and, if the time limit cannot be met in a specific case, the applicant shall be informed in writing of when the applicant may expect a decision. But there is no mention of where these deadlines are to be published. It is, of course, the case today that there is no requirement for the deadlines to be published on the local authorities' website, which is why it can be very difficult for citizens to find out. Some municipalities use the municipality's website to publish the deadlines, but not everyone does, as the municipalities can also choose other methods to inform citizens about the processing times. In order to make it easier for citizens to follow the case in the municipality, we in the Danish People's Party want the municipalities to be obliged to provide information about the processing times on the municipality's website. This uniformity will make it easy and fast for citizens to keep up. It must be made clear where citizens can find the deadlines. What is also important in this context is that citizens can inform themselves about the level of service provided by the municipalities in this area and about the length of time they can expect to have in the social field. It can be based on the municipality of Esbjerg, where it is easy and manageable to find the time limits for the proceedings, even in all areas where a municipal decision is taken. Other municipalities are more creative and they write, for example.: Waiting time from receipt to start of a case is from 0-6 months. It's not ideal. On 30 March 2015, the Ombudsman stated precisely on this issue that authorities should comply with and revise the time limits for the proceedings, and waiting for a response from a municipality in a social court case, it is not only entitled to a procedure as quickly as possible, but also to know how long to expect to wait. This particular proposal is not expected to entail extra costs for the municipalities, as they are already obliged to publish the deadlines somewhere, just not in a particular form or via a particular medium. It is our intention to make it easier for citizens, and I believe that we can all understand how frustrating it can be to have no clarity as to how long one's case can take in municipal affairs. We in the Danish People's Party believe that this resolution can increase legal certainty for citizens, and I am rather sad that there is no greater support for this resolution, because we have been asked about it. ParlaMint-DK_20150430162939	The debate is open. The Minister for Economic Affairs and the Interior.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430162954	We must now consider a resolution on one entry into the public sector via the community's citizens' service. The government, like the proposers, believes that citizens must experience a coherent public sector where it is easy to find the right entrance and where citizens face good and effective procedures. For most citizens, digital self-service solutions and digital mail are the natural gateway to communication with the public, and we can welcome the fact that almost 90 per cent. of citizens now have a digital mailbox. It provides a flexible everyday life where you can communicate with the public when you have time, and thus is not limited by the opening hours of the Citizen Service Centre. At the same time, digitalisation gives you the opportunity to spend money on welfare rather than postage and paper. But not all citizens are digital, and for those citizens who cannot operate digitally, it must remain easy to get in touch with the public. Here, the community's citizens' service will be the obvious one for many citizens. The authors of the proposals want the local community's citizens' service to be able to carry out all the types of tasks that citizens need to address to the municipality and the public sector, with the exception of civil registration. However, citizens already have the opportunity to obtain help to a large extent today by addressing the citizens' services of the municipalities. In addition to being the entrance to the public task areas, the municipalities' citizens' service provides general guidance and information on e.g.: the tax area and the opportunities available to obtain benefits from Payout Denmark, and citizens who have problems with NemID or wish to be exempted from digital mail may also contact the local community's citizens' service. For citizens with special needs, e.g. mental or physical disability, the municipality is obliged to maintain contact with other relevant authority on behalf of its citizens. Both SKAT and Payout Denmark have established special hotlines in this regard, so that the municipality can quickly get in touch with the relevant authority if for example. need a detailed guidance on the annual balance sheet, or if the municipality is to help a exposed citizen to obtain information on his or her case in Payout Denmark. This creates speedy and effective procedures, but above all security for citizens who have problems with digital solutions or who, moreover, have difficulty finding their way around the public service. It is important for the government that all citizens, digital and non-digital, experience a coherent public sector and that everyone can get the help they need. In recent years, the municipalities' portfolio of tasks has been constantly changed as a result of inter alia: the local government reform and the establishment of Payout Denmark. In some areas, therefore, tasks have been shifted in order to improve efficiency and to support an efficient and high-quality procedure. In Payout Denmark's territory, tasks have primarily been moved where it is not necessary for a case officer to make a specific estimate in the case. To a lesser extent, therefore, contact and dialogue with the citizen is needed, because the matter is primarily decided on the basis of objective criteria. Such tasks do not therefore need to be carried out in the geographical vicinity of citizens. In other types of task, it is appropriate to maintain the procedure in the municipalities. This applies, for example, when a citizen with disabilities is to be searched for a residence offer, and in other decisions, which are, of course, largely based on a concrete estimate. Therefore, it is important to have this distinction between types of tasks in mind in the discussion of where they are best solved. At the same time, it is not a secret that economic considerations are also included in the assessment of where tasks are best placed. Just as a single example I can mention about the financial gains by moving tasks that Payout Denmark today 2 years after commissioning has saved municipalities administrative expenses for approx. 300 million kr. annual. In addition, there are other further savings, for example. in the form of small IT licence costs. That is why we are constantly evaluating where the tasks are located in the various authorities, in order to ensure the proper performance of tasks in the public sector. Most recently, on 21 April 2015, with the broad support of all parties in the Folketing, with the exception of Enhedslisten, it was decided that five new areas of work will be transferred to Payout Denmark. The case handling in the five tasks is very similar to the tasks that Payout Denmark is performing today, and they also have a close connection with the current tasks. In addition, these are a few complex areas where it may be difficult for all municipalities to have the necessary powers to decide matters, for example. international health insurance cases. The change in law made it clear again that it is still the municipality's job to help citizens who need help with digital self-service in the five areas that Payout Denmark takes over and that it is also the municipality which must continue to be the link to Payout Denmark if it is a citizen with a special need who has difficulty in understanding how to call Payout Denmark himself. In order for citizens to experience more quality and coherence in the public sector, the government has an ambition that the next common public digitalisation strategy should ensure that future digitalisation is even more focused on creating value for citizens by relying on citizens' needs. This may sound elementary, but I think we can be better placed to focus public digitalisation on creating value for citizens and businesses for that matter. The majority of the objectives we have today for public digitalisation are directed inwards towards the public sector. There is therefore a need for us to be better placed to set targets aimed at achieving that citizens experience better service and higher quality. To ensure a better digital user experience, we need to look at non-digital citizens. What is it that makes some citizens have problems using NemID or certain self-service solutions and therefore show up at citizen service? We need to be better able to answer such questions and we need to find solutions that will respond to those issues. As has already been said, the government attaches great importance to the fact that all citizens meet a coherent public sector, whether digital or not. For some citizens, the citizen.dk is the natural entry to dialogue with the public, while the municipal civil service is the obvious once for others. Fortunately, today we have far along the way both well-functioning digital solutions and municipal citizens' service centres that can help and advise citizens who have problems with digital solutions. The municipalities' community service centres therefore serve to a large extent as a coherent entrance for citizens who need personal service. At the same time, the government considers that the current division of tasks between municipalities and, for example, the municipalities. SKAT and Payout Denmark is appropriate. It has been made on the basis of the balance between the need for an efficient public sector and the need to ensure that there is continued help in the municipality for those citizens who need assistance in the field of service, guidance or other face-to-face contact in connection with contact with the authorities. I also think that sometimes, and sometimes undeserved, the community's citizens' service gets a little bad reputation for the fact that it is always difficult and takes a long time and that you have to stand in line. My own experience just here recently is that it is very effective in many places. You can book a time, so you're free to stand in line. So even where people still have to attend, I believe that we have in fact organised a good and coherent service, whether citizens need new driving licences or passports, or more complicated procedures. Against this background, the government cannot agree to the motion for a resolution to change the organisation of the municipal civil service.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430164009	Thank you, Minister. Mrs Karen Ellemann as rapporteur for the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430164019	Since the rapporteur of the Left, Jan E. Jørgensen, was unable to be here right now, I have had the pleasure of acting as rapporteur in relation to this motion for a resolution by the Danish People's Party that we are debating. As the Minister has also mentioned, this is a motion for a resolution which deals with one entry into the public sector via the community's citizens' service. In concrete terms, therefore, the proposal is that citizens' services should now also be able to help citizens in relation to Payout Denmark and in relation to SKAT. I must be honest and admit that I have little difficulty in understanding some parts of the resolution, because it is already the case today that citizens' services have a duty to provide comprehensive guidance and to help non-digitally ready citizens and citizens with special needs when it comes to paying out Denmark. It is exactly in the law on Payout Denmark. It even states that the municipality must provide assistance to citizens who are deemed to have special needs in three areas. The first is to help fill in and submit an application to Payout Denmark; the second is to ensure that the citizen can participate in the processing of his or her case at Payout Denmark; and the third is to arrange the contact with Payout Denmark on behalf of the citizen. So, as the Minister also mentioned, help can already be given to citizens' services. And the Left estimates that the division of labour between Citizen Service and Payout Denmark, which is outlined in the Danish Payout Act, is quite fine. As far as the proposal that citizens' services should carry out tasks in relation to the SKAT is concerned, it is only to say that this proposal will actually counteract structural reform. For in 2005, SKAT was formed as part of the structural reform by merging the State Customs and Tax and Municipal Tax Administrations, thereby creating a nationwide unitary administration of tax recovery, and it is now organised in various central units and local tax centres. We decided in 2005 that tax collection and administration is a state matter, and that is why we do not actually see any reason to change it. Because if we are to be able to provide the services described in this motion for a resolution, it will mean that we are actually going to have to employ a great many people in civil service, because tax everything else is just a complicated area, and that is why it is still a matter of citizens having competent advice, and our assessment is that we get it from SKAT as it is today. So the conclusion of the rapporteur's speech will be that we will not be able to support this resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150430170805	Thank you. And thank you for the many comments. I actually hear a lot of support, even though the resolution will not ultimately be supported. With this proposal, we in the Danish People's Party want to oblige the government to take the initiative to ensure that the local community's citizens' services function as a coherent access to the public sector with the possibility of personal civil service in attendance. All citizens with enquiries and enquiries to the public, whether it concerns problems with using NemID or other questions, should be able to approach the municipality's citizens' service and be served and have their inquiries dealt with. That is what most rapporteurs are saying today: that is already the case. After all, we have talked a great deal about the legislation that has been adopted on Disbursement Denmark and on SKAT, on digitisation and on efficiency, in which we have talked a lot about ensuring, of course, that all citizens can get the help they need. To some extent, there is also the possibility that municipalities can help citizens. There are the direct numbers for Payout Denmark • that's right and it all sounds really good. But there is just the problem that when you get out in reality, to the citizens who had to be helped, there are far too many examples that the systems do not work, that the citizens do not feel helped. I hear from citizens who are told by the municipality that they can just call Payout Denmark or SKAT, and then they experience that they do not get through there. I hear from citizens who contact Payout Denmark for clarification of the rules, for example. when they are going on a longer stay abroad and may be uncertain about their pension relationship, then they talk to a person in Payout Denmark who gives them an explanation. A few days later, they have a follow-up question. When they call, they can't talk to the same employee they did last time, and that means they have to start over. And the message they get about the rules of the employee that they can then talk to is not the same as they got from the first employee. I hear a lot of such stories, and for each one of them, of course, it is easy to say that a solution can be found. Staff must have better reference systems, there must be shorter telephone waiting times, call information must be better documented, etc. etc. I also see some signs that a perhaps more service-minded culture could be worked on, and that, of course, should also be worked on. I just have to say that we in the Danish People's Party have come to the conclusion that in this way it will never be good enough. For the vast majority of us and all of us in here, digital systems and telephone contact will almost always be sufficient and yet: What do we do when it really burns on? Then, of course, we need real people to be able to get the dialogue that makes it possible to find out what to do and how to understand the process or the guidance or the decision. Today, fortunately, we still have civil service in the municipalities, although it has been severely cut as a result of the transfer of tasks to, among others. Disbursement Denmark. In the community service they can help with many things, but there are also many tasks where they may refer to Payout Denmark or SKAT, and then it is not good enough. We in the Danish People's Party believe that digitalisation can be used for a great deal, and of course we as a society must also use the tools available, but we should not go so far as to create a society that does not contain human contact and where we sit behind each of our small screens and manage everything. There I have to say two things: 1) There is and will always be a group for whom it will never be possible to operate a computer, and 2) it is actually not such a society I want. I want to live in a community where there is a physical door to the public nearby that I can walk in; behind that door there must be a real person with whom I can talk and who can really help me with what I need help with from the public. I want to live in a society where I can tell old Mrs. Hansen or young Peter that they can safely walk through the door of the municipality's civic service and expect there to be people in there who can and will help them all the way through. It's nice with technology and machines, and that I have a choice, wherever I am, to get my tax return or my application for housing benefits done, but it has to be my choice or we'll end up with an inhuman society. That is why we in the Danish People's Party say that the local community's citizens' service must be able to carry out all the types of tasks with which citizens need to approach the municipality and the public authorities. It will primarily be a relatively small group of citizens who cannot operate digitally, for example. by using NemID, which for this reason needs to be contacted in person. They may also be citizens who, despite being able to serve themselves via the Internet, are still left with questions that they would like to have answered or would like to have resolved by speaking face to face with an employee. In the Danish People's Party, we believe it is important that the citizens, many of whom already have anguish of contact with the public, who have come forward in civic service for help, should not just be passed on in the system to another body. Civil service must be the door to the public that citizens must use and where they can find the right, living people who must be able to remedy their problems. The Danish People's Party believes that the first step towards a solution could be to ensure that the municipalities have real access to the necessary information and systems so that the municipal citizens' service can help citizens through personal service. It must be ensured that the municipalities actually have the technical and competence possibilities to carry out the task as an intermediary between the citizen and, for example, the public. Payout Denmark or SKAT. So this does not mean that the case handling should be moved back to the municipalities, but the municipal employee should have the opportunity to contact SKAT, contact Payout Denmark on behalf of the citizen and clarify matters so that it is not the citizen who is forced to run between the agencies. The prerequisite for success is to ensure that the task is carried out in close and binding cooperation between municipalities, Payout Denmark and SKAT. As a starting point, Dansk Folkeparti believes that the task of the municipality must be solved with the resources already used today. To the extent that the efforts of the municipalities release resources at SKAT and Payout Denmark, they can be returned to the municipalities. This will be of benefit and benefit to the citizens, and that is why we have a public sector that must be of benefit and benefit to the citizens. Thank you.
