ParlaMint-DK_20150430172133	Thank you. Today's debate is, of course, about how we in Denmark should deal with the economic crisis that is in Europe and, in particular, about how we should deal with what is happening in the hard-hit countries and, above all, in Greece, which is, of course, a central debate in Europe right now. There are several reasons why this is an interesting debate. After all, the Danish economy is closely linked to the European economy, and it can be felt when several of our European neighbours are in deep economic crisis. The euro countries and, unfortunately, Denmark too, because of the financial pact, have increasingly become subject to the EU's economic governance and have followed the EU's crisis policy based on cuts in public spending. In recent years, this austerity policy has met with growing criticism because unemployment and social deprivation have exploded, while in many of the countries in crisis the debt ratio has risen. At the last Greek election, the Greek people opted out of this austerity policy. Rather than making savings, the desire was to find the money from the richest Greeks and to take action against corruption and tax havens that have plagued the Greek economy for decades. The new Greek Government has put forward a number of proposals for radical economic reforms and, in February, agreed with the Eurogroup. It was an interim agreement which, however, has since then met a great deal of criticism and opposition from both the European Commission, the European Central Bank and the International Monetary Fund, the IMF. The international institutions have criticised the reform plans of the Greek Government. Instead, these institutions wanted the Greeks to have pension reductions and continued savings made. This is of course interesting because today's debate is thus both about politics and about democracy. The question is whether we can get the wheels going in Denmark if there is recession and decline in many countries in Europe. And the question is whether the people around the EU Member States are allowed to choose economic policy in democratic elections and whether the people are allowed to opt out of the austerity policy desired in Brussels. It is a debate that rushes forward with full force around Europe, not least in other hard-hit countries in southern Europe and Ireland. Now the debate is also coming to the Folketing, and I look forward to hearing what the parties have to say to it.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430172428	Thank you to the rapporteur for the questions. Then it is the Minister for Economic Affairs and the Interior to answer the question. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430172440	I welcome the opportunity here today to debate Greece and, in a broader sense, the situation of vulnerable countries and the work to ensure growth and employment. I should also like to thank the authors of the question for having understood on an earlier occasion that we will only have the debate today. I would like to begin by saying that the current debate on the Greek elections is, of course, primarily about the way forward for the European loan to Greece, which has only been granted by the euro area countries. Denmark alone contributes through the IMF loan, which is why it is not for us in principle to give advice to euro area countries on how to deal with their lending. On 20 February the euro area countries concluded an agreement with the new Greek Government to extend the existing loan programme by four months until the end of June. This makes it possible to complete the outstanding review of the programme and to pay out outstanding funds to Greece. In the follow-up to the agreement with the Eurogroup, Greece has presented preliminary lists of the reform priorities it wishes to implement. It is, however, a prerequisite for payment under the programme that Greece should adequately implement these reforms, so that it is clear that they are making the necessary adjustments. This process and dialogue are continuing, and of course everyone hopes that it will lead to a sensible solution to the situation. However, the more fundamental debate today is probably about how our EU cooperation works and how we work together to tackle the crisis. I welcome this debate because it may give us the opportunity to resolve a number of misunderstandings. The first misunderstanding is that aid from the euro area countries to Greece should in itself be the problem. So it's turning things all the way upside down. Greece came out of its own hands in serious economic trouble in the context of the crisis. It had nothing to do with banks. More public money was spent on an ongoing basis than revenue and too little had been done to support sustainable growth and employment in the form of good framework conditions and sound structures. And in 2010 the situation became unsustainable and Greece could no longer finance itself on the market. This has to do with the fact that for a number of years the figures for the public deficit had been actually cheated, and that is not something that creates confidence anywhere. A major loan was granted to Greece. The aim was to give Greece time to recover and to avoid immediate adjustment, which had been very serious for Greek citizens. Without loans and market access, there would have been a need to strike an immediate balance between expenditure and revenue, and this would have meant far more drastic fiscal austerity. Greece lacked money, the euro area countries borrowed money from Greece, and I believe that this is an expression of great solidarity, just as it was an expression of a common interest in resolving the problems. The loan is self-help. It's not a grant. It must be temporary and therefore the loans require that underlying problems be resolved. A balance must be struck between public expenditure and revenue, and action must be taken to increase growth and employment potential. Has this resulted in paradise on earth in Greece? No, of course it hasn't. The Greek people have undergone considerable adjustments which are not enviable, but the situation would have been much worse without the loan programme. Another misunderstanding is that it should be wrong for the euro area countries to insist that the loan programme should continue in some form or other, given that there is a completely new government that wants something different from the old one. Let me make it clear that the Danish Government, like the other EU countries, naturally respects the outcome of the Greek elections and looks forward to working with the new government, which is already in full swing. However, Greece's economic challenges are the same, regardless of who forms the government, and the gap between revenue and expenditure is the same, and therefore the need for external financing is also the same. The basic conditions for the loan programme are, and it is also customary for new governments to stand by agreements made by previous governments, which were also democratically elected. This is also true here. After all, a new Danish government cannot ask for a line to be struck over public debt created by a previous government. The terms of the loan programme are not dictated by the euro area countries, by the ECB or the IMF. They are designed at the time in dialogue with Greece, and the emphasis can then be placed differently according to political preferences. It can be noted that the agreement of the euro area countries with the new Greek Government to continue the loan programme also provides for the possibility of changing measures into the programme, provided that they deliver the same effect and take place in dialogue with the other euro area countries. No one is preventing the Greek Government from putting even greater emphasis on combating tax fraud and corruption, on the contrary. It has always been part of the programme to combat corruption and ensure a proper tax system, and this has been something that the institutions, the Commission, the ECB and the IMF, and the other countries, have attached great importance to. If the new government is better at this than the old governments, the other euro countries will certainly take a positive view of the fact that there is now a government delivering what they have always proposed. The new Greek Government therefore has a good opportunity to make new or better efforts. A third misunderstanding is that Greece is now in a totally impossible and unsustainable situation, which there is no way out without debt relief. It's not obvious. Public debt is relatively high, but Greece's current repayment and interest payments are less than those of several other euro area countries, such as Greece. Italy and Portugal. This does not therefore constitute a separate problem for Greece. This is mainly because euro area countries' loans have low interest rates and very long repayment periods. Greece is much better placed to put the crisis behind it today than in the past on the basis of the loan programme. A precondition for Greece's progress will, however, be the consistent implementation of the ongoing adjustment. The new government has implemented fiscal easing and the roll-back of already implemented structural reforms, which point in the wrong direction and adversely affect debt sustainability. A relaxation of the fiscal policy in Greece also increases Greece's financing needs. As long as Greece has not built up sufficient confidence to finance itself on the market, the money can only come from the other euro countries. Some of the other euro area countries, which may be asked to finance Greece's fiscal easing and/or debt relief, are less prosperous than Greece, and some countries themselves have been through similar adjustments in the wake of the crisis. This applies, for example, to the Baltic countries, of which only Latvia received assistance from outside. The countries that have been through programmes and adjustments, such as Portugal, Ireland, Spain and the Baltic States, are currently among those where GDP growth and employment are highest. They have worked hard, as it were, to get in shape to catch up. Experience has shown that the more consistent and consistent a recovery is achieved, the safer the way back to renewed growth and employment. This also applies to ourselves in Denmark. The loan programme for Greece and other vulnerable EU countries is not alone. In the wake of the crisis, we in the European Union have carried out a number of important strengthenings of our economic cooperation. Much more attention has been paid to preventing situations where countries end up in such a mess as is the case in Greece. This applies, first and foremost, to the focus on sound public finances through the strengthened Stability and Growth Pact and the Financial Pact. However, this also applies to addressing other macroeconomic imbalances such as housing bubbles, loss of competitiveness, etc., which have also been a major part of the problem in several countries. A significant strengthening of financial regulation and supervision has been achieved and a banking union has been established. Finally, much better crisis management tools have also been developed in the form of the ESM loan facility of the euro area countries, and the ECB's monetary policy toolbox has been expanded with the possibility of buying out government bonds, etc. Most recently, the Commission has proposed a forward-looking investment plan which we adopted at ECOFIN in March and which is now being negotiated with the European Parliament. Together with the concrete implementation of growth-friendly consolidation and structural reforms in individual countries, these measures have, in my clear belief, helped to restore confidence and to support a dawning economic recovery. There are no easy solutions in Greece, at home or for the EU as a whole to ensure sustainable growth and employment. I sincerely hope that the Greek Government and the people will succeed and have the will to find a way forward with sustainable growth and employment within the framework of the European Union and euro cooperation, so that Greece too can gradually put the crisis behind it. The agreement between the Eurogroup and Greece on the extension of the recovery programme has so far provided the basis for Greece to formulate and implement the reforms essential for achieving sustainable growth and employment. It is therefore important that Greece now devotes its time wisely to doing just that, and that is what we are all currently waiting for them to do.

The Folketing supports the work of the EU to ensure measures that promote productive investment and economic sustainability, as well as compliance with EU fiscal rules. The Folketing expresses its full support for a sustainable solution to Greece's continuing economic challenges. It is in everyone's interest to find solutions that are sustainable both in the short and long term. The solution to Greece's economic challenges is primarily up to Greece itself within the framework of an agreement with the other euro area countries which, through large-scale loans, support Greece in a gradual and lasting adjustment of the Greek economy. • V 46).

ParlaMint-DK_20150430181308	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mrs Lisbeth Bech Poulsen, SF.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430181325	Thank you, President. There is no doubt that the Greek economy has long been extremely unhealthy. Greece only joined EMU through well-known statistics fraud, which many chose to overlook for political reasons, and the misery could to some extent be concealed during the recovery until 2008. Until then, Greece's interest rate was quite modest because the financial markets expected that the EU countries would eventually pay off Greece's debt if necessary. All this changed abruptly with the financial crisis. The reason for Greece's problems is that it is a weak state which cannot effectively levy taxes and with a widespread clientileism in which the two political wings have used, for example, in the past. public positions as rewards to supporters, and with extensive corruption. There is therefore no doubt that there is a need for significant reforms in Greece, and Syria is also fully aware of this. However, they attach more importance to effective tax collection and the fight against corruption than previous governments, and here it is a clear advantage that Syria has not been involved in corruption and clitentileism. They are more reluctant to raise their retirement age, to make the public sector more efficient and to carry out certain privatisations. It should be borne in mind here that, unlike in Denmark, privatisations can be an advantage in countries with extensive corruption. They have chosen to re-establish a social safety net which is to some extent equivalent to our cash assistance but at a lower level and will raise the minimum wage, which has been greatly reduced. The dispute between what must no longer be called the Troika and Greece is partly at the pace of the reforms and partly at the content. As far as the pace is concerned, the troika has insisted that Greece should reduce its national debt very quickly as a percentage of GDP. It is based on an idea that a country can quickly save itself for a better economy. Joseph Stiglitz, Nobel Prize winner and former Vice-President of the World Bank, have criticised strongly. On this point, I think the Greeks are right. There should be a more gradual phasing-in of reforms, etc., so as not to drain the economy from all demand. With regard to content, it seems that the troika has had a very strong focus on cuts in the number of public employees, minimum wages, social benefits, etc. and had less focus on the entire revenue side, perhaps because an effective tax system cannot be built in a short time and therefore does not fit in with the Troika's thinking about a quick fix. Whose fault is it then? Basically, the problem is created by the Greeks. The EU and the IMF have helped by easing the repayment profile of loans and new loans. They have made demands which are too primitive in terms of supply economic thinking, and also demands which, in my view and the SF, have far too much affected the weak and the poor and, far too modestly, the rich Greeks. On the other hand, there has also been a great deal of opposition in Greece to certain necessary reforms. Finally, the EU's general demand for a strict financial policy has helped to harm Greece. Juncker's investment plan, in my opinion, is still the emperor's new clothes, and if it has a little effect, it will in any case be far too late. SF's wish that all countries should invest extra, e.g. equal to 0,25%. of GDP would have much more impact and would work faster. The last thing I want to say is that how you look at the problem in the European Union, and especially the countries of southern Europe and, most specifically, Greece, depends also on how you look at the crisis and how it has arisen. In the SF's view, there was and is a structural problem with the euro. There has been and is simply too great a difference in how the economy is linked up in southern Europe and in northern Europe. That is also why we are not in favour of the euro. However, there is a group of euro countries which have granted loans to the Greeks. The Greeks have voluntarily entered EMU. They have even cheated and deceived their way in there, but in order to understand the crisis and to talk about the same thing, one also has to have some degree of agreement on what the underlying big problem is. If you do not, it is also difficult to point out the same solutions. However, in the eyes of the SF, there has been too much, too great a distinction between different countries to have the same monetary policy, and it has also come to the attention of a number of the countries that now say that then we must also have a common financial policy in Europe to a greater extent.

I was therefore also pleased that the Minister ended up confirming that the situation is that, after all, it is a balance between the political demands of the European Union on the Greeks and the political demands of other countries in crisis. In his first speech, the Minister said that he wanted the reforms to be concrete. That's quite interesting. I would urge the Minister to go back and talk to his officials about what specific reform requirements the Greeks have actually put on the table. To my knowledge, it is 70 very specific reform requirements, which are just not austerity policy. It is, of course, a political priority which the Greek people have to support. The situation is that where the European Union wanted to lend money to the former Greek Government, even though it knew that there were major problems with corruption and the lack of tax payments from not least the richest part of the population, it is now refusing to lend money to the new Greek Government because the government will not save on pensions and reduce labour market conditions. I find that thought-provoking. I think it is thought-provoking that the Minister is saying that the humanitarian recovery that the Greek Government has begun to make is apparently a move in the wrong direction. So it is a move in the wrong direction to take care of the most vulnerable and weak Greeks. I am very surprised at the optimism that exists in relation to other EU countries in crisis. We had earlier the debate on Latvia, which is now being presented as a great success. Well, it's a country where 20%. of the population has emigrated since the crisis. It was pointed out that Spain and Portugal will be very sad if, after making substantial savings, they see that Greece can go another way. Yeah, I can understand that. Those governments stand to be beaten by kodyle in the coming elections if it turns out that there is another way out of the crisis than they have gone through. But the reality is that both governments and, unfortunately, the European Union do not really want to have a discussion on what economic path can be pursued. You just want it to be the so-called necessary policy to be implemented, and everyone has to meet requirements that they themselves do not share in discussing. I think it is exciting that we have the debate today, not least because it is also about the Danish economy. We have a situation where there is an economic downturn on the way in England and the United States; the BRIC countries have started to go back economically; and we have a distressed economic situation in many of the European countries. It is, of course, relevant to discuss how we should get the wheels going at home if development is not reversed. If development is not reversed in these countries, where are we going to export our goods? That is why this debate is not just a debate about Greece or the countries in crisis. After all, it is also a debate on how to really get Denmark going in the future. I think it is amazing that we can have a situation in which an agreement can be reached between the Greek Government and the euro group in October, which involves radical reforms, and then we are working against the possibility of granting loans so that these radical reforms can be implemented. After all, it is thought-provoking that there must be pension reductions and reductions in professional rights on the table before money can be borrowed. It simply says that this is about politics, it is not about the economy, and that is where we have such political disagreements. However, I believe that the debate will continue, not least after some of the elections that are waiting around in other hard-hit countries in Europe, which may well choose to move towards Greece, and then we can have a more exciting debate. So thank you for the discussion today, and good May 1st to those who celebrate tomorrow, and good day off to the rest of you. Then I look forward to continuing the discussions when we come across the weekend.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430185838	There is just one brief comment. Sir. Helge Vagn Jacobsen.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430185841	Well, it has to be very short. I would also like to wish Mr. Nikolaj Villumsen. I just thought it was just a little strong tobacco here in the end to say that it went downhill in Europe. Fortunately, we have an increase in growth and a fall in unemployment, including in Denmark. It's a completely wrong picture that gets drawn there. It was just my only comment. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430185858	The rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430185859	It is true that there is a positive development in Denmark right now, and we should be very pleased about that, but I also think it is important to look a little forward. It has to be said that there is an economic downturn in Germany; there is an economic downturn in England; there is a situation in which many countries are still facing major problems, and I think we need to deal with this. There has been a strategy, not least on the part of the Danish Government, that it was hoped to be able to export to BRIK countries and thus get out of the economic crisis and create jobs. But when the BRIC countries go down economically, when the economy in Europe is not really going down, then we can soon get back into trouble at home. I think that is important, and that is why this debate is also real and concrete, in terms of how we ensure that the wheels get going in a lasting way, so that Danish jobs can be created.
ParlaMint-DK_20150430185954	Thank you to the rapporteur. As no one else has asked to speak, the debate is closed. The vote on the proposals for adoption submitted will take place only on Tuesday, 5 May 2015.
